5 Neural Computation

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Architecture stack focus for this chapter.

Why does understanding a neural network’s math matter more than reading its code?

Neural network code can look deceptively simple because a few tensor operations hide the workload that every layer of the system must execute. Matrix dimensions determine arithmetic and data movement; intermediate values determine memory pressure; numerical ranges and gradients determine whether learning remains stable. An operation that looks cheap in isolation can dominate a system when repeated across a batch or when its outputs must be retained for learning, so source-code length gives little indication of resource cost. Doubling both input and output dimensions quadruples a dense layer’s arithmetic, and keeping activations for the backward pass can multiply memory use several times over, while the code looks no more complicated than before. Two implementations of the same model can run very differently because batch shape, precision, and the target machine change what fits, how quickly it runs, and whether its calculations remain numerically valid. When training stalls or deployment exhausts memory, reading a framework call rarely identifies which resource is the binding constraint. A numerical failure, a memory bottleneck, and an inefficient implementation can produce similar high-level symptoms while demanding different remedies. Diagnosis and planning therefore require reasoning from the underlying operations to the bytes moved, values retained, and work repeated. This view can expose a mismatch before an expensive training run or deployment attempt does, when correcting it still costs an edit rather than a week of lost compute. Those primitives provide a stable vocabulary across architectures and make software abstractions auditable, turning opaque code into a workload that can be measured, diagnosed, and mapped to hardware. In D·A·M terms, neural computation is the interface where algorithmic structure becomes machine work and where physical limits feed back into model design.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain why learned arithmetic replaced rule-based logic and why it fails numerically rather than logically
  • Compare activation functions by nonlinearity, gradient behavior, and hardware execution cost
  • Calculate parameter counts, multiply-accumulate operations, activation storage, and the arithmetic intensity that batching raises in dense layers
  • Apply cross-entropy loss and gradient updates to reason about supervised learning dynamics
  • Derive forward and backward propagation as matrix operations over batches
  • Analyze training vs. inference workloads using compute, memory, and deployment constraints
  • Evaluate an end-to-end document-recognition pipeline from preprocessing through decision thresholds and human review
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

5.1 From Logic to Arithmetic

Recognizing one handwritten digit costs the small network at the center of this chapter 109,184 MACs. A multiply-accumulate (MAC) multiplies one weight by one input value and adds the product to a running sum (\(\MacAccumulator\leftarrow \MacAccumulator+ \ModelWeight\cdot \LayerInputComponent\)), and the usual convention counts it as two floating-point operations (\(1\text{ MAC} = 2\text{ FLOPs}\)), so the digit costs 218,368 FLOPs. None of that work is a logical branch. A program that once tested pixel thresholds and stroke patterns with if-then-else rules has become a fixed sequence of arithmetic. How fast it runs now depends on how quickly the machine can supply operands to that arithmetic, and so on whether memory bandwidth or arithmetic capacity runs out first. Such a program also fails differently. It rarely fails logically; it fails numerically, as values grow or shrink until they stop carrying information.

One example from the clean, versioned training set of Data Engineering cannot learn on its own. Crossing from the data pipeline into the model, the algorithm axis of D·A·M, hands that digit to mathematical operators that fix the work to be done and the state to be held. Under the silicon contract (principle 4), those operators supply the model’s half of the hardware bargain, while precision, data layout, implementation, and hardware fix how many bytes actually move and which machine resource saturates first.

↩︎ The hardware’s side of the silicon contract, the terms that memory bandwidth, peak compute rate, and fixed overhead set for any model, is established on the single-node stack in Inside one machine: The single-node stack.

The digit passes through five stages in the life of a model. The forward pass carries the image through three weight matrices to ten class scores. The loss reduces those scores to one number that says how wrong they are. The backward pass carries that signal back through the layers with the chain rule, and the update adjusts every parameter. Serving runs the forward pass alone with frozen weights. The iron law of ML systems (principle 3) charges each stage for the bytes it moves, the operations it performs, and its fixed overhead, and table 1 shows how differently the five stages spend those terms. The loss costs almost nothing, yet it is the only signal training receives; the update does little arithmetic but touches every parameter; the backward pass cannot run without the activations the forward pass left behind. Training repeats the first four stages millions of times and serving repeats only the first, and that asymmetry produces the chapter’s central result, the gap between what training and inference must hold in memory.

Example 5.1: Running example: MNIST digit recognition
Scenario: A handwritten-digit classifier receives \(28{\times}28\) grayscale images from the Modified National Institute of Standards and Technology (MNIST) dataset (LeCun et al. 1998), flattens each into 784 pixel values, and maps them through hidden layers of 128 and 64 units to 10 class scores, one per digit (784 → 128 → 64 → 10).

Diagnosis: The network holds 109,386 parameters, which occupy 438 KB in 32-bit floating point (FP32), and it spends 109,184 MACs (218,368 FLOPs) to classify one digit. Those three numbers summarize what one classification costs.

Systems lesson: The task is small enough to compute by hand yet exercises every mechanism that larger networks scale up. Each later section either derives one of these numbers or asks what happens to it when the network trains, grows, or ships.

Even this small network is deep in the sense the field uses. Each of its layers computes new features from the layer before, and training learns those features from examples instead of receiving them from a designer.

Definition 5.1: Deep learning

Deep learning is the computational paradigm that learns layered feature representations from data by composing nonlinear transformations. For suitable tasks, those learned representations can reduce manual feature engineering while increasing computation \((\Operations)\) and state.

  1. Significance: For suitable compositional function classes, depth can represent a hierarchy much more parameter-efficiently than a shallow network. Each layer adds work and state, and the resulting binding constraint may lie on the data, algorithm, or machine axis depending on the workload.
  2. Distinction: Shallow methods such as logistic regression and support vector machines can work well with appropriate representations. Deep learning instead uses multiple nonlinear stages to learn representations jointly with the prediction task, which can reduce manual feature design and sometimes support transfer across tasks.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that parameter count alone explains deep learning. Two networks with the same parameter count can differ sharply in what they learn, because architecture, data, optimization, and scale together determine the practical result.

Learning representations end to end also introduces engineering failure modes that conventional software debugging cannot isolate. Deep learning failures produce subtle symptoms: gradient instabilities1 that halt convergence, numerical precision errors that corrupt parameter state across millions of updates, or inefficient memory access patterns in tensor operations (operations on multidimensional arrays of numbers) that leave accelerator compute units underutilized. Resolving these failures requires reasoning about the physical execution of tensor algebra on hardware rather than stepping through procedural control flow. Those hardware demands depend first on representation, that is, on whether patterns are expressed through hand-authored rules, engineered statistical features, or learned parameters.

1 Gradient instabilities: In a simplified 20-layer path through sigmoid (S-shaped) activations, the activation-derivative factors alone are bounded above by \(0.25^{20}\), or \(9.1 \times 10^{-13}\). The complete gradient also includes weights and other operations, but repeated saturated derivatives can still make early-layer updates negligible. Plateaus or not-a-number (NaN) values can reveal the symptom without identifying the cause; rectified linear unit (ReLU) activations and careful initialization (both taught later in this chapter), together with normalization, better optimizers, and residual connections, all helped make deep networks trainable, and Skip connections and gradient flow treats residual connections in detail.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An engineer deploys the same neural network model on an accelerator with high GPU memory and on an older accelerator with smaller memory capacity. The model executes cleanly on the larger card but crashes mid-batch on the smaller card with a CUDA out-of-memory error on an intermediate activation tensor, without generating a Python traceback. Per the Silicon Contract perspective of neural computation, which diagnostic approach addresses the root cause of this failure?

    1. Audit the Python application code and framework version to identify an unhandled control-flow software exception.
    2. Increase logging verbosity to capture a Python traceback, assuming the crash stems from glue-code syntax errors.
    3. Downgrade the GPU driver and CUDA toolkit under the assumption that the newer driver introduced backward-incompatibility bugs.
    4. Calculate the intermediate activation and parameter memory requirements directly from layer tensor dimensions, because the mathematical operators—not the application code—determine the physical memory demands.

  2. Recognizing one MNIST digit requires approximately \(109{,}000\) multiply-accumulate operations. Explain why a traditional control-flow debugger is insufficient when such a network fails, and describe how its failures differ from those of a rule-based program.

  3. True or False: In the iron law of ML systems (\(T = \frac{D_{\text{vol}}}{\text{BW}} + \frac{O}{R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}}} + L_{\text{lat}}\)), the shift from rule-based logic to dense neural computation primarily increases the network latency term \(L_{\text{lat}}\) because matrix multiplications introduce distributed network round-trips.

See Answers →

5.2 Computing with Patterns

Before a learned network spent 109,184 MACs on that \(28{\times}28\) digit, earlier generations of computing attacked the same classification problem with two simpler representation strategies. An illustrative rule tree tests roughly one hundred pixel comparisons per digit; an engineered feature pipeline performs several thousand arithmetic operations; the three-layer neural network performs more than one hundred thousand multiply-accumulates. Each strategy replaced a predecessor whose representations ran out of expressive reach on unconstrained handwriting, and each step up in representation capacity shifted which hardware resource bound execution.

↩︎ The succession of logic, knowledge, feature-engineering, and infrastructure bottlenecks that these paradigms answered is established in The Evolution of AI Bottlenecks.

5.2.1 From rules to engineered features

Writing explicit if-else rules to recognize a handwritten digit breaks as soon as a writer tilts a stroke, thickens a line, or leaves a loop open. Rule-based programming requires developers to specify every procedure that maps inputs to outputs: in the classical flow of figure 1, human-authored rules and input data enter the program together, and the program deterministically computes the answers.

An illustrative rule-based classifier for the digit compares pixel intensities against fixed thresholds, checks stroke patterns in localized regions, and branches on the results. Under this toy implementation, the computation requires roughly 100 comparisons over 784 bytes of pixel data, a workload that is small, predictable, and easily held in an ordinary CPU cache. The comparison establishes a workload baseline, not the accuracy of a production rule system. Rules fail on coverage rather than cost: every new writing style demands new branches, and the expert systems of the 1970s and 1980s stalled on perception tasks because visual competence is implicit and resists explicit rules.

The failures of rule-based systems suggested an alternative. Rather than encoding human knowledge as explicit rules, the system could discover patterns from data, and machine learning took that direction by replacing a rule for every situation with programs that identified patterns in examples. The success of these methods, however, still depended heavily on human insight to define which patterns to look for, a process known as feature engineering.

Feature engineering transformed raw data into representations that expose patterns to learning algorithms. The histogram of oriented gradients (HOG)2 (Dalal and Triggs 2005) exemplifies this approach. In figure 2, the feature-extraction pipeline divides an image into spatial cells and bins local pixel gradient directions into eight orientation histograms, turning raw pixels into shape descriptors that tolerate lighting changes and small shifts in position.

2 Histogram of oriented gradients (HOG): An influential handcrafted descriptor for pedestrian detection before deep learning (Dalal and Triggs 2005). One common configuration computes gradient orientations in \(8{\times}8\) pixel cells, with cell size and other choices tuned for the task. HOG’s fixed computation graph can run efficiently on CPUs with predictable latency, while learned features can reduce descriptor redesign but do not automatically transfer across domains and often benefit from accelerators only as model and batch scale grow.

Dalal, Navneet, and Bill Triggs. 2005. “Histograms of Oriented Gradients for Human Detection.” 2005 IEEE Computer Society Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR’05) 1: 886–93. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2005.177.

Feature engineering raises the digit’s cost while keeping it CPU friendly. For the same \(28{\times}28\) digit, an illustrative HOG implementation divides the image into a 7 by 7 grid of 4 by 4 cells and computes gradient magnitudes and orientations. It bins them into 9 orientation histograms per cell (the diagram draws eight directions; the bin count, like the cell size, is a tuning choice) and produces a 441-element feature vector. A linear support vector machine (SVM) classifier then performs ten dot products over that vector. The result is roughly 8,000 operations and about 2 KB of working memory, about 80× the work of the toy rule-based baseline. The work is still structured and well served by CPU vector units using single instruction, multiple data (SIMD). Descriptor size, class count, and implementation all change this cost.

These descriptors carried face and pedestrian detection through the 2000s, but every new problem needed an expert to design a new extractor, and the resulting features missed whatever patterns their designers had not anticipated. Human expertise, not computation, set the ceiling on how much real-world visual variation a system could absorb.

5.2.2 Automatic pattern discovery

Neural networks remove that ceiling by discovering their own features, learning representations directly from raw data instead of following explicit rules or human-designed extractors. Supervised learning inverts the traditional programming relationship. Engineers provide examples and target answers, and optimization produces the decision rules as output rather than taking them as input (figure 3). Humans still shape the task through data, objectives, architectures, and evaluation.

The same digit, processed by the chapter’s three-layer network (784 → 128 → 64 → 10), costs 109,184 MACs, 1,100× the work of the toy rule-based baseline. The 109,386 parameters occupy 438 KB in FP32, more than most L1 caches hold, so even inference creates traffic between cache levels. Training multiplies the cost further. Each image is processed forward and backward before an update, at two to three forward passes of dense-layer arithmetic, repeated over 60,000 images for multiple epochs (full passes over the training set). Dense matrix multiplications now dominate the arithmetic and favor parallel hardware, shifting the engineering challenge from control-flow branching to memory bandwidth, data reuse, and hardware parallelism.

In this MNIST comparison, learned features spend far more arithmetic.

This trade is the bitter lesson at the scale of a single layer. HOG encodes an expert’s knowledge of edges and orientations in 8,000 operations that never change; the learned first layer spends far more arithmetic and lets the data decide which pixel combinations matter. As computation became cheap enough, the general method that converts arithmetic into accuracy overtook the hand-built one. That victory demands the arithmetic, memory traffic, and training infrastructure that the rest of this chapter quantifies.

↩︎ Why general methods that scale with computation keep overtaking methods built on human knowledge, the bitter lesson, is established in The Bitter Lesson.

The escalation matters less for its 1,100× operation count than for what each step does to the binding resource, which moves from branch-heavy CPU control, to batch-oriented feature pipelines, to memory-fed matrix parallelism (table 2). Dense matrix work meets the memory wall, the widening gap between compute throughput and off-chip memory bandwidth, whenever an operation performs too little arithmetic per byte it moves. That ratio is the arithmetic intensity introduced alongside the iron law; the forward-pass analysis later in this chapter measures it for the running network, and Performance Bounds develops it into a full performance bound.

↩︎ The memory wall and the physical reasons that off-chip bandwidth lags arithmetic are established in Physical Constraints on Deployment.

↪ The AI memory wall, the point at which added FLOP/s stops reducing runtime because data movement dominates, is developed in The AI memory wall.

Scale therefore has costs as well as gains. Larger models and training budgets turn arithmetic throughput, memory bandwidth, storage capacity, and communication into potential bottlenecks, and model size is not a decision rule. Choosing a model requires comparing candidates on representative deployment data and on the complete cost of training, serving, monitoring, and maintenance, with the uncertainty in both gains and costs included. The useful question is whether the measured gain from additional scale earns those system costs.

↪ How model size, training data, and compute trade off along a compute-optimal frontier is developed in The compute-optimal frontier.

Systems Perspective 5.1: When to use neural networks

Not every problem benefits from deep learning. Stage 1: Problem Definition established that a problem definition must require a learned system to improve on an existing baseline by more than its added cost. Table 3 lists the conditions under which a neural network tends to win that comparison, and table 4 the conditions under which a classical method tends to match it with less training, serving, and maintenance effort.

Systems insight: Establish a simple baseline before building a neural network, and retain the neural approach only when its measured benefit justifies the added system. The document-recognition history in section 5.6 shows why the comparison must include preprocessing, rejection policy, and the cost of human review rather than model accuracy alone.

5.2.3 Why backpropagation waited for systems

Every increase in a neural network’s size multiplies its forward operations, saved activations, gradient computations, and parameter updates, which is why backpropagation waited decades for hardware and data to catch up with the algorithm. Paul Werbos applied backpropagation3 to neural networks in 1974 (Werbos 1974), building on Seppo Linnainmaa’s 1970 work on automatic differentiation (Linnainmaa 1970), and Rumelhart, Hinton, and Williams showed in 1986 that hidden units could learn useful internal features (Rumelhart et al. 1986). That demonstration did not resolve the systems cost exposed by the digit classifier. The decades between early backpropagation work and widespread adoption required the maturation of parallel hardware, large datasets, weight initialization, non-saturating nonlinearities, and software frameworks. An algorithm can be viable in principle long before its surrounding system makes it useful in practice. The lesson does not run in reverse: a computationally expensive idea is not vindicated by expense alone, and it must be evaluated together with the data, software, hardware, and task regime that would make it useful.

3 Backpropagation: Short for “backward propagation of errors,” the algorithm uses the chain rule to compute how the loss is locally sensitive to millions of weights in one backward traversal. Its systems cost is memory rather than arithmetic, because the backward pass needs the forward-pass activations, which training must store alongside gradients and optimizer state, the several-times-larger footprint quantified in section 5.4.5.5.

Werbos, Paul. 1974. “Beyond Regression: New Tools for Prediction and Analysis in the Behavioral Sciences.” PhD thesis, Harvard University.
Linnainmaa, Seppo. 1970. “The Representation of the Cumulative Rounding Error of an Algorithm as a Taylor Expansion of the Local Rounding Errors.” Master's thesis, University of Helsinki.
Rumelhart, David E., Geoffrey E. Hinton, and Ronald J. Williams. 1986. “Learning Representations by Back-Propagating Errors.” Nature 323 (6088): 533–36. https://doi.org/10.1038/323533a0.

↩︎ How AlexNet, GPUs, and ImageNet ended the feature-engineering era, and why infrastructure became the next bottleneck, is established in The deep learning era and the infrastructure bottleneck.

Checkpoint 5.1: Understanding deep learning's emergence
Before turning to the primitives that set these costs, verify your understanding of why deep learning emerged when it did:

If any of these concepts remain unclear, review the relevant sections before continuing.

The digit classifier’s cost and the adoption lag lead to the same conclusion. What a network costs the machine is fixed by the primitives it is built from, and those primitives are the subject of the rest of this chapter.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team replaces a hand-coded digit recognition system (\(\approx 100\) conditional comparisons, \(784\text{ bytes}\) of working state) with a \(784 \to 128 \to 64 \to 10\) MLP (\(\approx 109{,}184\text{ MACs}\), \(\approx 438\text{ KB}\) of weights) on the same MNIST input. Which systems consequence should they expect when deploying this new model on a commodity CPU?

    1. The workload becomes dominated by branch mispredictions because artificial neurons execute frequent if-then branching decisions.
    2. The workload transitions from branch-heavy scalar code to dense matrix arithmetic whose weight footprint exceeds L1 cache capacity, generating cache-level memory traffic absent in the rule-based system.
    3. Execution memory traffic drops to zero because the \(438\text{ KB}\) weight footprint fits entirely within standard CPU register files.
    4. The model executes without using arithmetic logic units (ALUs) because learned representations bypass hardware compute pipelines.

  2. A hardware vendor claims that increasing single-threaded CPU clock frequencies by \(5\times\) will eliminate the necessity of specialized accelerators (such as GPUs or TPUs) for deep neural networks. Based on the computational profile of neural networks, what is the strongest technical refutation of this claim?

    1. Deep learning is dominated by massive parallel matrix multiplications bounded by arithmetic throughput and memory bandwidth, which benefit from thousands of parallel SIMD/tensor units rather than higher scalar clock speeds.
    2. Higher CPU clock frequencies force the loss function to become non-convex, destabilizing gradient descent.
    3. Modern neural networks require asynchronous analog circuits that cannot be simulated on digital CPU cores.
    4. Increasing CPU clock frequencies reduces the numerical precision of floating-point registers to 4-bit integers.

  3. A computer vision team evaluates two approaches for a multi-class recognition task: (a) a handcrafted feature pipeline (e.g. HOG + SVM) and (b) an end-to-end convolutional neural network. Compare the systems engineering trade-offs between these two approaches when the product must scale from 2 initial categories to 50 diverse object categories.

  4. Explain the systems-level concept of the ‘algorithm-hardware adoption lag’ using the historical trajectory of backpropagation (Werbos 1974 / Rumelhart et al. 1986) and modern deep learning. Which surrounding factors had to mature before the algorithm achieved widespread practical viability?

  5. True or False: For a tabular dataset with 500 records and linear relationships between features and targets, deploying a deep neural network is generally superior to a linear regression model because deep networks inherently generalize better across all problem scales.

  6. Order the pattern-computing paradigms as the chapter presents their progression, from explicit logic to learned representations: (1) Deep learning with automatic hierarchical feature discovery on parallel accelerators, (2) Rule-based programming with explicit hand-authored logical branches, (3) Classical machine learning pairing hand-engineered feature extractors (e.g. HOG) with statistical classifiers.

See Answers →

5.3 Neural Network Fundamentals

Each of the 109,184 MACs that classify a single digit belongs to some neuron combining its inputs. Individually those operations are trivial. At network scale, a processor spends more time waiting for operands than computing whenever its memory system cannot supply them fast enough. An accelerator such as a graphics processing unit (GPU) outruns a CPU on suitable neural workloads because it devotes silicon to wide arithmetic arrays and high-bandwidth memory rather than to branch prediction. The structure of the network primitives determines how data flows through that hardware and how memory demands scale during training and inference.

Those primitives (weighted sums, nonlinear activations, and gradient-based learning) anchor the feed-forward networks of this chapter and recur inside the larger architectures that later chapters introduce, which add operators and execution patterns this simple model does not contain. Parallelism and the memory wall, the pressures traced through the paradigm comparison, follow from that structure. Many simple units operate simultaneously on shared inputs, weights change during training, and layers compose low-level features into high-level ones. That structure begins with the artificial neuron.4 Understanding it plays the role for a neural network that understanding a single transistor plays for a processor.

4 Neuron: McCulloch and Pitts (1943) introduced a mathematical threshold model of nervous activity: inputs combine and an all-or-none output fires when a threshold is met. That logical neuron is an origin point for the artificial-neuron abstraction and for networks built from many simple units. Learned weights, deep hierarchies, and modern fused multiply-add (FMA) accelerator datapaths are later developments that turn this abstraction into the matrix-heavy workloads studied in this chapter.

McCulloch, Warren S., and Walter Pitts. 1943. “A Logical Calculus of the Ideas Immanent in Nervous Activity.” The Bulletin of Mathematical Biophysics 5 (4): 115–33. https://doi.org/10.1007/bf02478259.

5.3.1 One neuron

To test whether a \(28{\times}28\) image contains a particular stroke, a single computing unit must weigh all 784 pixel intensities, sum the evidence, and decide how strongly to fire. That basic unit of neural computation, the artificial neuron (or node), began as a simplified mathematical model of nervous activity (McCulloch and Pitts 1943) before digital implementations turned it into a standard arithmetic primitive. Figure 4 sets the biological cell and its mathematical counterpart side by side. The left panel draws a biological cell, with branching dendrites that receive signals, a cell body that holds the nucleus, and an axon that carries the output away. The right panel draws the mathematical model. Its inputs \(\LayerInputComponent_1\), \(\LayerInputComponent_2\), and onward, plus a constant input of one that carries the bias, flow along weighted edges into a node. The node first forms a linear sum \(\NeuronPreactivation\) and then applies an activation function \(\ActivationFunction\) to emit the output \(\NetworkOutput\). The column of labels between the panels pairs each biological structure with its arithmetic counterpart: dendrites with inputs, synapses with weights, the soma (cell body) with the linear sum, and axon firing with the activation.

Read from left to right, the right panel of figure 4 carries one signal through four stages, and each stage takes its name from one label pair in the column between the panels:

  1. Input Reception (Dendrites → \(\LayerInputComponent_1, \LayerInputComponent_2, \dots, \LayerInputComponent_n\)): The column of circles on the left of the panel is the vector of input features \(\InputFeatureVector\) that the artificial neuron receives. In MNIST digit recognition, these represent pixel intensities. The dendrite analogy captures reception of many signals without claiming biological equivalence.
  2. Weighted Modulation (Synapses → \(\ModelWeight_1, \ModelWeight_2, \dots, \ModelWeight_n\)): Each input is multiplied by a learnable weight \(\ModelWeight_i\), just as synaptic strengths modulate biological signals. These weights act as “gain” controls, determining how much influence each feature has on the final decision. A bias term \(\NeuronBias\) (shown as the top input \(\LayerInputComponent_0 = 1\) in figure 4) shifts the activation threshold. Across a network, learned behavior is distributed over many such parameters rather than stored in one weight.
  3. Signal Aggregation (Cell Body → Linear Function \(\NeuronPreactivation\)): The left half of the split node, labeled \(\NeuronPreactivation\), integrates the weighted signals, producing a single scalar value \(\NeuronPreactivation= \sum (\LayerInputComponent_i \cdot \ModelWeight_i) + \NeuronBias\). This mirrors how a biological cell body sums incoming electrochemical signals to determine whether the neuron has received enough evidence for a particular pattern.
  4. Nonlinear Activation (Axon → Activation Function \(\ActivationFunction\)): In the right half of the node, labeled \(\ActivationFunction\), the aggregated signal passes through an activation function \(\ActivationFunction(\NeuronPreactivation)\) and leaves along the output arrow, producing the activation output \(\NeuronActivation= \ActivationFunction(\NeuronPreactivation)\) (often denoted \(\NetworkOutput\)). This mirrors the axon’s all-or-nothing firing decision, since the nonlinearity determines whether the neuron “fires” a signal to the next layer. Unlike the biological case, \(\ActivationFunction\) can produce graded outputs (ReLU, for example, passes positive values through and zeroes negatives), but the principle of thresholding followed by propagation is the same.

For the machine, one neuron costs \(\LayerInputWidth\) MAC5 operations. A direct scalar evaluation loads \(\LayerInputWidth\) inputs, \(\LayerInputWidth\) weights, and a bias before writing one output, so without reuse it moves about two values per MAC. Batched and tiled kernels reuse inputs and weights across many neuron evaluations and cut that traffic per MAC, which is why performance at network scale depends on how well the implementation reuses data as much as on how many operations it performs. Once neurons are composed into a network, three constraints appear that a single unit hides. Inside any one layer, neurons can evaluate in parallel, yet moving their parameters and activations across memory often limits throughput before arithmetic capacity runs out. Across layers, layer \(\LayerIndex+1\) cannot start until layer \(\LayerIndex\) finishes, placing a sequential floor under end-to-end latency. Numerical precision cuts across both limits, because narrower formats move fewer bytes and run on denser arithmetic units, provided task quality survives the search for minimum viable precision that Model Compression develops. The perceptron formalization in section 5.3.2.1 turns the four stages into the equations that this accounting counts.

5 MAC (multiply-accumulate): Hardware data sheets report fused multiply-add throughput in FLOP/s, so a quoted FLOP/s rate is twice the corresponding MAC/s rate. Layer and batch choices also create activation, communication, and data-movement costs, so MAC count is necessary but not sufficient for a systems budget.

5.3.2 From neurons to layers

A single artificial neuron can only score one weighted combination of its inputs, so recognizing handwritten digits requires composing hundreds of units into layers whose collective activation choices fix the computational workload. In the running network of Example 5.1, every unit in a layer runs the same scalar inner product before choosing which nonlinear activation to pass forward.

The perceptron’s weighted sum

In the first hidden layer of the MNIST network, a single unit combines all 784 pixel intensities into one score that responds to a learned stroke pattern, such as a vertical edge shared by “one” and “seven.” That unit is the perceptron,6 which Rosenblatt introduced as a trainable form of the artificial neuron (Rosenblatt 1958) along the signal path traced in section 5.3.1. Inside one unit (figure 5), input features \(\LayerInputComponent_i\) travel along weighted channels \(\NeuronConnectionWeight\) and accumulate into a scalar sum with bias \(\NeuronBias_j\), and the resulting linear score \(\NeuronPreactivation_j\) passes through an activation \(\ActivationFunction\) to produce output \(\PredictedOutputComponent\). Repeating that calculation across 128 units with different learned weights lets the first layer test 128 candidate stroke patterns against the same image in parallel.

6 Perceptron: A machine built to execute a learning algorithm, directly linking hardware and software from the start. A single linear-threshold layer cannot represent nonlinearly separable functions such as XOR, regardless of additional input hardware (Minsky and Papert 1969). Multilayer nonlinear networks expand the representable function class, but they also introduce the training and systems costs developed in this chapter.

Minsky, Marvin, and Seymour A. Papert. 1969. Perceptrons: An Introduction to Computational Geometry. The MIT Press.
Rosenblatt, Frank. 1958. “The Perceptron: A Probabilistic Model for Information Storage and Organization in the Brain.” Psychological Review 65 (6): 386–408. https://doi.org/10.1037/h0042519.

Each input \(\LayerInputComponent_i\) has a corresponding weight \(\NeuronConnectionWeight\), and the perceptron multiplies each input by its matching weight. The intermediate output, \(\NeuronPreactivation_j\), is computed as the weighted sum of inputs in equation 1: \[ \NeuronPreactivation_j = \sum_i (\LayerInputComponent_i \cdot \NeuronConnectionWeight) \tag{1}\]

Here, \(\NeuronIndex\) identifies the receiving neuron once perceptrons are assembled into a layer, and the sum runs over all input indices \(\InputFeatureIndex\). In plain terms, each input feature is scaled by how important it is (its weight), and the results are summed into a single score. This is the dot product of two vectors.

A bias term \(\NeuronBias_j\) shifts the linear output up or down, giving the model additional flexibility to fit the data. Thus, the intermediate linear combination computed by the perceptron including the bias becomes equation 2: \[ \NeuronPreactivation_j = \sum_i (\LayerInputComponent_i \cdot \NeuronConnectionWeight) + \NeuronBias_j \tag{2}\]

With this weighted sum in hand, a suitable output activation and loss can support regression or classification. A binary linear-threshold classifier selects one of two classes around a threshold; multiclass networks instead produce several scores and commonly normalize them into probabilities with softmax, an output function developed with the other activation functions below.

The formalization turns the neuron’s cost into a layer’s. A dense layer of \(\LayerOutputWidth\) neurons repeats the weighted sum \(\LayerOutputWidth\) times, so its arithmetic cost is \(\LayerInputWidth \times \LayerOutputWidth\) MACs. That cost is exactly the matrix multiplication \(\InputFeatureVector\WeightMatrix\) that hardware must execute, and it is the form in which the rest of this chapter counts work. The dot product is the operation that accelerators are built to execute at maximum throughput, which is why neural network performance is quoted in MACs or FLOPs completed per second. How those machines are organized around it is developed in Matrix operations in neural networks. A weighted sum alone, however, can only draw straight boundaries, and the activation applied to it decides what the layer can represent.

Nonlinear activation functions

Without a nonlinearity between layers, a stack of linear weight matrices collapses algebraically into a single linear transformation and can only draw straight decision boundaries, no matter how many intermediate matrices the machine evaluates. Activation functions break that collapse by converting each neuron’s linear weighted sum into a nonlinear output, making them the point where expressiveness, gradient flow, and hardware cost meet. Applying an activation \(\ActivationFunction\) to the weighted sum of equation 2 yields the generic artificial-neuron computation in equation 3: \[ \PredictedOutputComponent_j = \ActivationFunction(\NeuronPreactivation_j) = \ActivationFunction\left(\sum_i (\LayerInputComponent_i \cdot \NeuronConnectionWeight) + \NeuronBias_j\right) \tag{3}\]

In figure 6, a network without activation functions is constrained to a straight decision boundary (left panel) that misclassifies overlapping point clusters, whereas nonlinear activations allow curved boundaries (right panel) that separate the two classes cleanly. The plot illustrates representational capacity rather than an optimization guarantee; training dynamics and sample coverage still govern whether optimization finds that boundary.

XOR is the classic function that no single weighted sum can compute, and its four input pairs show why a hidden nonlinear layer changes what a network can represent.

Example 5.2: Building intuition: The XOR problem
Consider a network learning the XOR function, a classic problem that requires nonlinearity. With inputs \(\LayerInputComponent_1\) and \(\LayerInputComponent_2\) that can be 0 or 1, XOR outputs 1 when inputs differ and 0 when they are the same.

Setup: Two inputs → two hidden neurons → one output

Example: With hidden units \(\HiddenUnitOutput_1 = \text{ReLU}(\LayerInputComponent_1 + \LayerInputComponent_2)\) and \(\HiddenUnitOutput_2 = \text{ReLU}(\LayerInputComponent_1 + \LayerInputComponent_2 - 1)\), the output \(\NetworkOutput= \HiddenUnitOutput_1 - 2\HiddenUnitOutput_2\) gives 0, 1, 1, 0 for inputs \((0,0)\), \((0,1)\), \((1,0)\), \((1,1)\). The input \((1,1)\) drives both hidden units, and the second one cancels the first. No single weighted sum of \(\LayerInputComponent_1\) and \(\LayerInputComponent_2\) can produce that pattern.

Systems insight: Hidden layers do not merely add capacity; they change the class of functions the system can represent. XOR, which no single-layer perceptron can compute (Minsky and Papert 1969), is the small example that makes depth a structural requirement rather than a decorative design choice.

7 Universal approximation theorem: Under conditions on the activation, target function, compact domain, and approximation tolerance, a sufficiently wide single-hidden-layer network can approximate the target arbitrarily well (Cybenko 1989; Hornik et al. 1989). This existence result is nonconstructive: it does not specify how to find the weights or how many neurons practical training will require. Why depth can nonetheless beat width is the subject of section 5.3.5.

Cybenko, George. 1989. “Approximation by Superpositions of a Sigmoidal Function.” Mathematics of Control, Signals, and Systems 2 (4): 303–14. https://doi.org/10.1007/bf02551274.
Hornik, Kurt, Maxwell Stinchcombe, and Halbert White. 1989. “Multilayer Feedforward Networks Are Universal Approximators.” Neural Networks 2 (5): 359–66. https://doi.org/10.1016/0893-6080(89)90020-8.

The XOR network is the smallest case of a general result. The universal approximation theorem7 establishes that sufficiently wide networks with suitable activation functions can approximate broad classes of functions arbitrarily well. The theorem, however, states a pure existence result under the assumption of unlimited width; it says nothing about the accelerator executing the computation. ML systems impose hard limits on both dimensions of the width-vs.-depth trade-off. A single hidden layer wide enough to approximate a complex function requires parameter storage and activation memory that can exhaust the accelerator’s device memory (VRAM) entirely. A network too deep to fit its activations in memory stalls the backward pass on the same constraint. The engineering problem is therefore not whether a network of sufficient width or depth exists, but whether it fits within the memory and bandwidth envelope of the target hardware.

Comparing activation functions requires one idea from training, which section 5.4 develops fully. Training adjusts each weight by its gradient, a number measuring how much the prediction error would change if that weight moved slightly. That signal travels backward through the network, a pass called backpropagation, and is multiplied at each layer by the activation’s slope, so an activation whose slope is near zero passes almost no learning signal to the layers before it.

The choice of activation function therefore affects both learning and computational efficiency. ReLU8 (\(\max(0, \NeuronPreactivation)\)) became a common hidden-layer activation for feed-forward networks because it is inexpensive, has local derivative one for positive inputs, and can produce sparse activations (Nair and Hinton 2010; Glorot et al. 2011). Earlier differentiable deep networks often used sigmoid or tanh. Their smooth S-curves saturate, a failure the sigmoid discussion below develops. That contrast is not a simple ranking: sigmoid remains appropriate for some binary outputs, tanh remains useful when bounded zero-centered activations are desired, and modern architectures use additional activation families. The relevant design question is which function supplies the required output semantics and gradient behavior at an acceptable execution cost. Figure 7 charts the response curves and output ranges of three standard element-wise nonlinearities alongside the vector-level softmax function, contrasting their saturation regimes.

8 ReLU (rectified linear unit): ReLU’s cheap comparison-and-select evaluation and its non-saturating positive branch made deep networks markedly faster to train; in the AlexNet paper, a four-layer convolutional network reached 25 percent training error on CIFAR-10 six times sooner with ReLU than with tanh (Krizhevsky et al. 2012).

Krizhevsky, Alex, Ilya Sutskever, and Geoffrey E. Hinton. 2012. “ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 25.
Nair, Vinod, and Geoffrey E. Hinton. 2010. “Rectified Linear Units Improve Restricted Boltzmann Machines.” Proceedings of the 27th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML-10), 807–14.
Glorot, Xavier, Antoine Bordes, and Yoshua Bengio. 2011. Deep Sparse Rectifier Neural Networks. International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS).
Sigmoid

The logistic sigmoid function9 squashes continuous inputs onto the open interval \((0,1)\) (equation 4): \[ \SigmoidFunction(\NeuronPreactivation) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\NeuronPreactivation}} \tag{4}\]

9 Sigmoid: From Greek sigma + eidos (“sigma-shaped”), referring to the S-curve that maps inputs to the bounded (0, 1) range. The mapping requires an exponential approximation, commonly implemented through specialized instructions, lookup tables, or polynomial methods. It is generally more expensive than ReLU’s comparison-and-selection operation, although the measured gap depends on precision, vectorization, and hardware. ReLU’s adoption reflected both easier gradient flow on its positive branch and cheaper execution.

The S-shaped curve maps logits, the raw unnormalized scores a layer produces, to \((0,1)\), so sigmoid can parameterize a binary probability. It approaches one for large positive inputs and zero for large negative inputs. Sigmoid is smooth and differentiable everywhere. Gradient-based learning does not require that smoothness; ReLU, introduced below, has a corner at zero, and implementations simply assign it a slope there.

Sigmoid’s main limitation is saturation. For inputs with large absolute values (far from zero), its gradient becomes extremely small, a phenomenon called the vanishing gradient problem (Bengio et al. 1994). Even at its peak, sigmoid’s derivative is only 0.25, so each sigmoid layer can shrink the activation-derivative part of the gradient by at least a factor of four. During backpropagation these small activation derivatives multiply with weight-dependent factors across layers, the compounding that the chapter’s opening footnote evaluated over 20 layers. Repeated saturation can make early-layer gradients and weight updates negligibly small, preventing useful learning along those paths.

Bengio, Yoshua, Patrice Simard, and Paolo Frasconi. 1994. “Learning Long-Term Dependencies with Gradient Descent Is Difficult.” IEEE Transactions on Neural Networks 5 (2): 157–66. https://doi.org/10.1109/72.279181.
Tanh

The hyperbolic tangent10 centers output activations around zero over the interval \((-1,1)\) (equation 5): \[ \tanh(\NeuronPreactivation) = \frac{e^\NeuronPreactivation- e^{-\NeuronPreactivation}}{e^\NeuronPreactivation+ e^{-\NeuronPreactivation}} \tag{5}\]

10 Tanh (hyperbolic tangent): By centering its output range on zero, tanh can reduce the correlated-update behavior associated with all-positive activations. Its computational cost is similar to sigmoid because it also depends on exponential approximations, while its saturation behavior still permits vanishing gradients.

Tanh produces an S-shaped curve similar to sigmoid but centered at zero: negative inputs map to negative outputs and positive inputs to positive outputs, a symmetry that balances gradient flow during training and often yields faster convergence than sigmoid. Like sigmoid, tanh is smooth and differentiable everywhere, and it saturates near -1 and 1, so it shares the vanishing gradient problem for inputs with large magnitudes. Even so, its zero-centered outputs make it preferable to sigmoid in recurrent neural networks, which reapply the same layer at every step of a sequence and must keep activations balanced across time steps. Both S-curves therefore carry two costs, saturation during training and an exponential evaluation in hardware, whereas ReLU removes saturation for positive inputs and replaces the exponential with a comparison.

ReLU

ReLU sets negative values to zero while maintaining linear identity for positive inputs (equation 6): \[ \text{ReLU}(\NeuronPreactivation) = \max(0, \NeuronPreactivation) = \begin{cases} \NeuronPreactivation& \text{if } \NeuronPreactivation> 0 \\ 0 & \text{if } \NeuronPreactivation\leq 0 \end{cases} \tag{6}\]

ReLU’s characteristic shape, a straight line for positive inputs and zero for negative inputs, provides three advantages. First, its local derivative is one for positive inputs, avoiding activation saturation on that branch; the complete network gradient can still shrink or grow through weights and other operations. Second, ReLU introduces deterministic activation sparsity by setting negative values to zero. Third, ReLU uses a comparison and selection, output = (input > 0) ? input : 0, rather than an exponential approximation. These are local properties of the operator, while end-to-end performance depends on how it is fused with neighboring work.

ReLU is not without drawbacks. The dying ReLU problem occurs when neurons become stuck in the inactive state, permanently outputting zero and ceasing to learn. If a neuron’s weights evolve during training such that the preactivation \(\NeuronPreactivation= \InputFeatureVector\WeightVector+ \NeuronBias\) is consistently negative across all training examples, the neuron outputs zero for every input. Since ReLU’s gradient is also zero for negative inputs, no gradient flows back through this neuron during backpropagation. The weights cannot update, and the neuron remains dead. This can happen when the learning rate, the step size that scales each weight update, is large enough to push weights into unfavorable regions. Careful initialization (He et al. 2015), moderate learning rates, leaky ReLU variants, and normalization layers11 help mitigate dying ReLUs by keeping preactivation distributions well scaled.

11 Batch normalization: Normalization layers (Ioffe and Szegedy 2015) stabilize intermediate activations across training batches, permitting higher learning rates and reducing the risk of dying ReLUs. Normalization and activation stability examines the trade-offs among normalization variants, and Compression-specific fusion shows how batch normalization folds into adjacent weights for inference.

Ioffe, Sergey, and Christian Szegedy. 2015. “Batch Normalization: Accelerating Deep Network Training by Reducing Internal Covariate Shift.” Proceedings of the 32nd International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML) 37: 448–56.
Softmax

Unlike element-wise activation functions that operate independently on each value, softmax12 is a vector-level function that considers all values together to produce a probability distribution. In simple classifiers, softmax is commonly used in the output layer rather than as an element-wise hidden activation; it also normalizes learned importance-score vectors in attention architectures. The softmax function is defined in equation 7: \[ \text{softmax}(\NeuronPreactivation_i) = \frac{e^{\NeuronPreactivation_i}}{\sum_{j=1}^\ClassCount e^{\NeuronPreactivation_j}} \tag{7}\]

12 Softmax: The name, coined by Bridle (1990), reflects its role as a “soft” or differentiable version of argmax, a function that must evaluate an entire vector to find its maximum value. This vector-wise operation is not used as a drop-in replacement for element-wise nonlinearities such as ReLU, but it is central whenever a model must normalize a vector of scores, including classification heads and attention layers. Direct exponentiation can overflow in 32-bit floating point, so stable implementations subtract the largest logit first (section 5.4.3.3).

Bridle, John S. 1990. “Probabilistic Interpretation of Feedforward Classification Network Outputs, with Relationships to Statistical Pattern Recognition.” In Neurocomputing: Algorithms, Architectures and Applications, edited by Françoise Fogelman Soulié and Jeanny Hérault, vol. 68. NATO ASI Series f: Computer and Systems Sciences. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-76153-9_28.

13 Logits: In multiclass neural classifiers, logits are unnormalized output scores. Score differences correspond to log probability ratios after softmax, while an individual multiclass logit is not itself a calibrated log-odds quantity. Because argmax over logits and argmax over softmax probabilities select the same class, inference can skip softmax when only the top class is needed.

For a vector of \(\ClassCount\) values (often called logits13), softmax transforms arbitrary real-valued scores into \(\ClassCount\) normalized predicted probabilities that sum to 1, although those values are not necessarily calibrated confidence estimates. As plotted in the bottom-right panel of figure 7, individual softmax components grow exponentially across logit values while the denominator couples all vector elements so that the class with the largest logit receives the highest probability. Because this mapping from logits to probabilities is differentiable, gradients flow cleanly back through softmax during training, and combining softmax with cross-entropy loss (section 5.4.3) yields simple gradient expressions that guide learning effectively.

Activation choice also carries a silicon cost, a transistor tax paid in circuit complexity, area, and switching energy. A ReLU datapath needs only a sign-bit comparator and a multiplexer, roughly 50 transistor equivalents. An exponential approximation for sigmoid or tanh is modeled here as a stylized budget of 2,500 transistor equivalents, 50× the comparator, an order-of-magnitude teaching ratio rather than a constant across manufacturing nodes. Logic area is only half the cost. An element-wise activation run as its own kernel is limited by memory traffic rather than arithmetic, a gap the forward-pass analysis later quantifies, so compilers fuse it into the preceding matrix multiplication. ReLU’s backward pass also needs only one bit per unit, recording whether the unit was active. A dead ReLU unit still occupies weight memory and still costs MACs on every inference while contributing nothing to the output, and ReLU’s zeros save no work in a dense kernel unless sparse hardware or routines exploit them. One neuron is now complete, and the next question is how many of them run side by side.

Sigmoid’s exponential datapath dwarfs ReLU in transistor-equivalents (illustrative).

Layers and connections

Neurons run side by side in layers, collections of units that execute in parallel across shared inputs, and the power of neural networks comes from that organization rather than from any single unit. An input layer ingests raw feature tensors; one or more hidden layers transform those representations through successive matrix multiplications and nonlinear activations; and an output layer emits task-specific predictions or decision logits.

In the feedforward schematic of figure 8, the task fixes only the two ends. The input layer’s width matches the four input features and the output layer’s width matches the two predictions. The hidden layers between them can widen and narrow freely, which is why hidden width is a design decision rather than a property of the data. Each hidden layer applies a parameterized linear transformation followed by an element-wise nonlinearity, progressively constructing higher-level abstractions from raw input signals.

As data flows through the network, it is transformed at each layer to extract meaningful patterns. Each layer applies the neuron’s weighted sum and activation in parallel across all its neurons, and its outputs become the next layer’s inputs. Simple features detected in early layers combine into increasingly complex patterns in deeper layers.

On the machine, a layer is one matrix multiplication over all of its neurons at once, and the boundary between layers is where data moves and waits. Each layer writes its output as an activation tensor that the next layer must read. Inference can discard that tensor once the next layer has consumed it, and training must keep it until the backward pass. The next layer cannot start until that tensor exists, which is the source of the latency floor that no amount of parallel hardware inside a layer removes. Figure 9 traces that translation in three steps. One neuron is a dot product of \(\LayerInputWidth\) MACs. Stacking a layer’s neurons and a batch of \(\BatchSize\) examples (inputs processed together in one pass) turns those dot products into one matrix multiply of \(\BatchSize\cdot\PreviousLayerWidth\cdot\CurrentLayerWidth\) MACs (for the first MNIST layer at batch 32, \((32{\times}784)\cdot(784{\times}128)\)). The hardware executes that multiply by holding a tile of weights in an array of multiply-accumulate units while rows of inputs stream past, so each weight fetched from memory serves every example in the batch. What each of those multiplications reads besides the activations is the layer’s parameters.

5.3.3 Parameters

Each of those multiplications reads the layer’s learnable parameters,14 its weights and biases, alongside the incoming activations. The parameters decide which transformation a layer applies, and their organization fixes both what the network can learn and how many bytes every pass must move.

14 Parameter memory cost: Parameter count is a misleading proxy for memory importance. The normalization layers that Normalization and activation stability introduces, for example, add a few learned scale and shift parameters per layer that barely affect byte budgets but can strongly affect model behavior when training choices change. Conversely, the bulk of parameters in dense weight matrices require extra state during training: gradients plus optimizer bookkeeping beyond the stored weights themselves. A model that fits in memory for inference may therefore require several times more memory for training, a cost quantified later in the training memory budget.

Weight matrices

In a dense layer with \(\LayerInputWidth\) input features and \(\LayerOutputWidth\) neurons, the weights form a matrix \(\WeightMatrix\in \mathbb{R}^{\LayerInputWidth \times \LayerOutputWidth}\) whose column \(\NeuronIndex\) holds neuron \(\NeuronIndex\)’s weights, so the layer computes all \(\LayerOutputWidth\) preactivations as the single product in equation 8: \[ \PreactivationVector= \InputFeatureVector\WeightMatrix+ \BiasVector \tag{8}\]

The canonical case studied here connects each neuron in a layer to every neuron in the previous layer, forming a “dense” or “fully connected” layer. This pattern allows each neuron to use all features from the previous layer. Fully connected layers establish the mathematical principles needed here, while alternative connectivity patterns exploit structure in the data and can reduce work or improve statistical efficiency.

Figure 10 details this dense interconnect topology on a concrete two-layer network (\(3 \to 4 \to 2\) units). Layer counts in this chapter count layers of weights, not layers of units, so this network has two and the MNIST network (\(784 \to 128 \to 64 \to 10\)) has three. The figure labels every scalar weight \(\NeuronConnectionWeight\) and layer bias \(\NeuronBias_j\) alongside intermediate node activations. Reading the incoming edges for any single destination neuron isolates one column of the weight matrix, while evaluating all destinations in parallel forms the complete matrix-vector multiplication. The numerical values illustrate parameter placement rather than an active inference pass; the essential systems property is the matrix dimension. For layers of widths \((\LayerWidth_0, \LayerWidth_1, \LayerWidth_2)\), with \(\LayerWidth_0\) the input width, the weight matrices span \(\WeightMatrix^{(1)} \in \mathbb{R}^{\LayerWidth_0 \times \LayerWidth_1}\) and \(\WeightMatrix^{(2)} \in \mathbb{R}^{\LayerWidth_1 \times \LayerWidth_2}\), requiring \(\LayerWidth_0\LayerWidth_1+\LayerWidth_1\LayerWidth_2\) weights before biases.

These products account for most of the work in a dense feed-forward network; whether they are compute bound or memory bound depends on their dimensions, batch size, precision, implementation, and target hardware. The layer looks elementary, yet the same dense layer is the feed-forward block inside every transformer layer and the towers of the deep learning recommendation model (DLRM), so the arithmetic counted here is the arithmetic those lighthouses spend. A transformer layer whose feed-forward block widens to four times the model width \(\HiddenDimension\) holds \(8\HiddenDimension^2\) feed-forward weights against \(4\HiddenDimension^2\) in its attention projections (the four square weight matrices that prepare the layer’s attention inputs and combine its outputs), so the feed-forward block holds 67 percent of the layer’s weights. In the GPT-2 lighthouse, with \(\HiddenDimension\) = 1,600, that is about 20.5M of each layer’s 30.7M weights. Network Architectures develops both architectures in full.

Bias terms

Alongside its column of weights, each neuron in a layer carries one more parameter, a bias term15. For a layer with \(\LayerOutputWidth\) neurons, these parameters form a vector \(\BiasVector\in \mathbb{R}^{\LayerOutputWidth}\) added to the weighted sum in equation 8: while weights determine the relative importance of inputs, each entry of \(\BiasVector\) shifts its neuron’s activation threshold independently at the cost of one addition per output value. The arithmetic is negligible, but its placement is not. Run as a separate step, the bias addition would read the layer’s entire output tensor from memory and write it back for one addition per value. Optimized libraries therefore apply it, together with the activation, while each output tile is still on chip at the end of the matrix multiply.

15 Bias terms: Biases add one parameter per neuron, compared with \(\LayerInputWidth\) weights for a neuron with \(\LayerInputWidth\) inputs, so they account for \(1/(\LayerInputWidth+1)\) of that layer’s parameters. Their model-wide share and effect on accuracy depend on layer widths, architecture, and normalization. Some modern architectures omit biases in layers followed by batch normalization because the normalization transform includes a learned shift.

The organization of weights and biases across a feed-forward network follows a systematic pattern. For each parameterized affine layer \(\LayerIndex\), three components define its computation; the weights and biases are learned, while the activation function is usually chosen by the designer:

  • A weight matrix \(\WeightMatrix^{(\LayerIndex)}\)
  • A bias vector \(\BiasVector^{(\LayerIndex)}\)
  • An activation function \(\ActivationFunction^{(\LayerIndex)}\)

This yields the complete layer computation in equation 9: \[ \ActivationVector^{(\LayerIndex)} = \ActivationFunction^{(\LayerIndex)}(\PreactivationVector^{(\LayerIndex)}) = \ActivationFunction^{(\LayerIndex)}(\ActivationVector^{(\LayerIndex-1)}\WeightMatrix^{(\LayerIndex)} + \BiasVector^{(\LayerIndex)}) \tag{9}\]

Here, \(\ActivationVector^{(\LayerIndex)}\) (written as \(\ActivationMatrix^{(\LayerIndex)}\) for batches) represents the layer’s activation output. The row-vector convention is adopted throughout: each sample is a row, and the weight matrix \(\WeightMatrix^{(\LayerIndex)} \in \mathbb{R}^{\PreviousLayerWidth \times \CurrentLayerWidth}\) maps from the previous layer’s width to the current layer’s width. With equation 9, one layer is fully specified, and what remains is choosing the layers themselves: how many, how wide, and how connected.

5.3.4 Choosing a topology

A single layer of neurons cannot even compute XOR (Example 5.2), yet adding or widening hidden layers to gain capacity immediately multiplies the parameters and activations the machine must store and move. Network topology, how neurons organize into layers and connect across them, fixes both what the network can represent and what it costs to run. The chapter’s MNIST digit recognizer shows how problem structure fixes the input and output dimensions while leaving the hidden layers as a systems design choice.

Feedforward network architecture

For the running MNIST classifier, the data format and task fix the two ends of the three-layer architecture before any hidden-layer design begins. In figure 11, Panel (a) sets a \(28{\times}28\) grayscale image of a “7” beside the input units and ten digit outputs (0–9), while Panel (b) shows how those 784 pixels flatten into a contiguous one-dimensional column vector before entering the first dense layer. Because a fully connected layer assigns an independent weight to every coordinate of that 784-element vector, flattening discards two-dimensional pixel adjacency altogether. Convolutional architectures instead wire spatial locality directly into the layer. That structural assumption is an inductive bias (here, that nearby pixels belong together), which restricts the functions a model can learn toward those suited to images (Mitchell 1980). Making almost no spatial assumption leaves the fully connected network to learn pixel relationships from data alone, at the price of a weight for every input-to-hidden pair.

Mitchell, Tom M. 1980. The Need for Biases in Learning Generalizations. CBM-TR-117. Rutgers University, Department of Computer Science.

↪ How spatial locality and weight sharing replace the parameter overhead of fully connected layers is developed as a spatial inductive bias in CNNs: Spatial Pattern Processing.

Between the fixed 784-unit input layer and the 10-unit logit layer, the number of hidden layers and their widths remain open engineering choices. Adding layers increases depth so that successive transformations can compose simple edges into strokes and digit parts, whereas widening a layer expands how many distinct features the network can represent at the same level of abstraction.

Widening both hidden layers from 100 to 1000 neurons, comparing a \(784 \to 100 \to 100 \to 10\) network with a \(784 \to 1000 \to 1000 \to 10\) network, increases parameters from ~89.6K to ~1.8M (~358 KB vs. ~7 MB in FP32). Whether the wider network improves accuracy requires training and evaluation; the storage increase alone already matters for mobile deployment budgets.

Layer connectivity design patterns

Widening both hidden layers jumped the parameter count by an order of magnitude because the architecture of section 5.3.3 connects every unit in one layer to every unit in the next. Connectivity itself is an architectural choice: dense, sparse, and skip patterns trade all-to-all flexibility against parameter count, spatial locality, and gradient flow.

In dense connectivity, each unit receives a weight from every unit in the preceding layer, which is why wiring 784 inputs to 128 hidden units requires 100,352 weight parameters (\(784{\times}128\)) in the first layer alone. All-to-all wiring lets a layer learn arbitrary linear combinations of its inputs (extended to nonlinear relationships by the activations between layers) and maps directly onto the dense matrix-multiplication kernels that hardware accelerators execute best. Because parameter count grows as the product of adjacent layer widths, however, widening one layer scales parameters linearly while widening both scales them quadratically.

Sparse connectivity cuts that parameter growth by restricting each neuron to a subset of the inputs in the adjacent layer, much as biological neurons connect only to nearby cells. In visual processing tasks like MNIST, neurons might connect only to nearby pixels to reflect the local nature of visual features. Although sparse connections reduce theoretical parameter counts and FLOPs, a sparse weight matrix does not automatically execute faster than a dense one on standard hardware. Skipping arbitrary zeros requires storing index metadata and performing indirect memory lookups to match each surviving weight with its input, which breaks contiguous tensor streaming and stalls wide matrix units built for fixed dense blocks. Unless the hardware supports a regular, structured sparsity pattern that its kernels can predict (developed in N:M structured sparsity mechanics), the arithmetic reduction does not translate into a proportional reduction in execution time.

Whereas sparse connections prune links within a layer, skip connections add direct paths between nonadjacent layers (He et al. 2016) to keep deeper networks trainable. As networks grow deeper, multiplying derivatives across many layers can shrink the backward gradient before it reaches the early weights. A skip connection routes an earlier layer’s activation tensor around one or more intermediate layers and adds it directly to a later layer’s output. Because addition has a derivative of one, the backward error signal can travel straight across that shortcut without being attenuated by the intervening weight matrices and activation slopes, while the later layer can still combine earlier features with newly computed ones.

Model size and computational complexity

Every connectivity choice ends in a parameter count. How parameters (weights and biases) are arranged determines both learning capacity and computational cost. The parameter count, its numerical precision, and the operations it requires are the concrete terms of the model’s side of the silicon contract. Parameter count grows with network width and depth, and table 5 lays it out for the running network layer by layer.

The first layer alone holds 100,352 of the 109,184 weights. Because a dense layer performs one multiply-accumulate per weight for each input, the same table also counts the arithmetic per image, and the parameter and MAC columns differ only by the 202 biases. At four bytes per FP32 value, the 109,386 parameters occupy 438 KB. Each must be stored in memory, read on every forward pass, and updated during learning, and the training and inference memory estimates later in this chapter start from this table rather than recounting it. What the table does not yet explain is why the network splits those parameters across three narrower layers instead of placing them in a single wide one.

5.3.5 Why depth matters

A single-layer network attempting to classify handwritten digits must map all 784 raw pixels directly to ten class labels in one step, relearning every stroke pattern separately for each digit. The running network spreads its weights over three layers instead so that deeper layers can exploit compositionality, in which complex patterns decompose into simpler patterns that themselves decompose further. In image recognition, learned representations can progress from local edges and textures toward parts and objects; such hierarchical regularities occur in many datasets and motivate layered models, although a trained network need not recover this exact sequence.

Consider recognizing the digit “seven” in the MNIST example. Rather than mapping all 784 pixel values directly to a decision, a deep network can compute the decision more parameter-efficiently by reusing intermediate features:

  • Layer 1 learns simple edge detectors: vertical lines, horizontal lines, diagonal strokes
  • Layer 2 combines edges into shapes: the horizontal top stroke of a “seven,” the diagonal downstroke
  • Layer 3 combines shapes into complete digit patterns

Each layer builds on the previous, allowing later computations to reuse earlier features. The same edge detectors learned for “seven” can also contribute to recognizing “one,” “four,” and other digits. This parameter reuse helps explain why depth is an effective design choice without implying a fixed parameter advantage for every task. The choice between adding layers and widening existing ones is not symmetric, however, because depth and width contribute to representational capacity through different mechanisms and cost the machine different things.

Systems Perspective 5.2: The depth vs. width trade-off
For certain function classes, depth provides an exponential representational advantage: a network with \(\LayerCount\) layers can represent functions that require exponentially more units in a shallow network (Telgarsky 2016). This restricted theoretical result explains why nonlinear composition can be parameter-efficient; it is not a universal guarantee for every task.

Modern architectures balance depth against width. A network with 10 layers of 100 neurons has the same 1,000 total hidden units as one with 2 layers of 500, but not the same parameter count, function class, or execution graph. Both expose parallelism within each layer; the deeper network adds sequential dependencies and may represent some compositional functions more efficiently.

Telgarsky, Matus. 2016. “Benefits of Depth in Neural Networks.” Proceedings of the 29th Annual Conference on Learning Theory, 1517–39.

The cost side of that trade-off follows from counting. For a fully connected network with layer widths running from \(\LayerWidth_0\) (the input) to \(\FinalLayerWidth\) (the output), each layer \(\LayerIndex\) holds a \(\PreviousLayerWidth{\times}\CurrentLayerWidth\) weight matrix and \(\CurrentLayerWidth\) biases, and its forward pass performs one MAC per weight for every example. The training pass must also retain each layer’s output for every example in a batch of \(\BatchSize\), so the parameter count \(\Parameters\), the forward operations per example \(\Operations\) (two FLOPs per MAC), and the retained activation memory \(\ActivationMemory\) follow equation 10: \[ \Parameters = \sum_{\LayerIndex=1}^{\LayerCount} (\PreviousLayerWidth + 1)\,\CurrentLayerWidth, \qquad \Operations = 2\sum_{\LayerIndex=1}^{\LayerCount} \PreviousLayerWidth\,\CurrentLayerWidth, \qquad \ActivationMemory \propto \BatchSize \sum_{\LayerIndex=1}^{\LayerCount} \CurrentLayerWidth \tag{10}\]

When every hidden layer has the same width \(\HiddenDimension\), the sums collapse. Parameters and operations grow as \(\LayerCount\HiddenDimension^2\), linear in depth and quadratic in width, while retained activations grow as \(\BatchSize\LayerCount\HiddenDimension\), linear in both. Figure 12 plots the two sweeps on the running network’s input and output widths. Holding the network at two hidden layers and doubling their width from 2,048 to 4,096 multiplies MACs per example by 3.4×, short of four only because the input layer’s share still grows linearly. Holding the width at 512 and doubling depth from 16 to 32 hidden layers multiplies MACs by 2.0× and retained activations by 1.9×.

Depth and width add capacity on different terms. Depth adds capacity at linear cost in parameters, operations, and activation memory. Every added layer is also another step that must finish before the next begins. Depth therefore raises the latency floor of a single example, lengthens the list of activations that training must hold until the backward pass, and lengthens the path a gradient must survive on its way back to the first layer. Width adds capacity at quadratic cost in parameters and operations (activation memory still grows only linearly). All of that added work lands inside one matrix multiplication whose rows and columns can be spread across parallel hardware, so a wider layer keeps an accelerator busy without adding a sequential step. Choosing between them is therefore a choice between paying in serialized steps and memory or paying in arithmetic. The batch size \(\BatchSize\), which multiplies only the activation term, decides how heavily the memory side weighs during training.

Checkpoint 5.2: Neural network architecture fundamentals
Before proceeding to training, verify your understanding of the foundational concepts:

Use the MNIST architecture 784 → 128 → 64 → 10 as the running check.

If any of these feel unclear, review the earlier sections on neural network fundamentals, artificial neurons, and parameters before continuing. The upcoming sections on training and optimization build directly on these foundations.

Network architecture, neurons, and parameters are now in place, but nothing yet explains how randomly initialized parameters become useful. Architecture answers what the model computes; training answers how it learns. The mechanics reveal systems constraints because training adds backward computation, saved activations, gradients, and optimizer state. Batch size then becomes a joint statistical and hardware choice, because it changes gradient noise, activation memory, data reuse, and parallel efficiency at once.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In a fully connected multilayer perceptron with layer dimensions \(784 \to 128 \to 64 \to 10\), which layer represents the primary parameter storage and computational hotspot, and what is its weight parameter count?

    1. The \(64 \to 10\) output layer with \(640\) weights, because the softmax denominator forces quadratic parameter expansion.
    2. The \(128 \to 64\) hidden layer with \(8{,}192\) weights, because it bridges intermediate representations.
    3. The \(784 \to 128\) input layer with \(100{,}352\) weights (\(784 \times 128\)), accounting for over \(91\%\) of the network’s total weight parameters and forward MACs.
    4. All three layers have identical parameter counts because linear algebra kernels require static square matrix padding.

  2. From a hardware and systems engineering perspective, why has the Rectified Linear Unit (\(\text{ReLU}(z) = \max(0, z)\)) largely replaced Sigmoid (\(\sigma(z) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-z}}\)) in deep hidden layers?

    1. ReLU makes many activations exactly zero, which by itself guarantees dense end-to-end training speedups on standard hardware and prevents gradient decay through the whole network.
    2. ReLU uses a simple comparison/select operation instead of exponential-based computation, and its derivative is \(1\) on the positive branch, which avoids local saturation there even though full-network gradients still depend on weights and other layers.
    3. Sigmoid and ReLU have essentially the same execution cost on modern hardware, so the preference for ReLU comes mainly from its bounded output range \([0,1]\).
    4. Because ReLU has lower local compute cost than sigmoid, switching activations alone always reduces end-to-end training time regardless of the rest of the model or input pipeline.

  3. Explain why stacking multiple linear layers without nonlinear activation functions (\(\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{x}\mathbf{W}_1\mathbf{W}_2\mathbf{W}_3\)) fails to increase the expressive capacity of a neural network, and describe what changes once nonlinear activation functions are inserted between the layers.

  4. True or False: For compositional target functions where complex patterns decompose hierarchically into reusable sub-patterns, deep networks can represent some functions much more parameter-efficiently than shallow networks, and in restricted function classes that advantage can be exponential.
